An elaborate new proof of Cayley’s formula
Esther Banaian, Anh Trong Nam Hoang, Elizabeth Kelley, Weston Miller, Jason Stack, Carolyn Stephen, Nathan Williams

Abstract
. We construct a bijection between certain Deodhar components of a braid variety constructed from an affine Kac-Moody group of type A n − 1 A_{n-1} and vertex-labeled trees on n n vertices. By an argument of Galashin, Lam, and Williams using Opdam’s trace formula in the affine Hecke algebra and an identity due to Haglund, we obtain an elaborate new proof for the enumeration of the number of vertex-labeled trees on n n vertices.



\addtotheorempostheadhook

[theorem]

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Introduction

It is well-known that the following sets have size
n^{n-2}
:

•

\textsc{tree}_{n}
, the set of vertex-labeled trees with
n
vertices
[
Cay89
]
(
Cayley’s formula
); and

•

\textsc{fact}_{n}
, the set of factorizations of the long cycle
(1,2,\ldots,n)
in the symmetric group
S_{n}
into a product of
(n-1)
transpositions
[
GJ97
]
.

But finding a bijection between
\textsc{tree}_{n}
and
\textsc{fact}_{n}
is surprisingly tricky (for a discussion, we refer the reader to the excellent paper
[
GY02
]
; see also
[
Sta97
]
).

It turns out to be much easier to show that

(n-1)!|\textsc{tree}_{n}|=(n-1)!|\textsc{fact}_{n}|.

We quickly sketch the bijection. The factor
(n-1)!
on the left-hand side comes from labeling the
n-1
edges of a vertex-labeled tree bijectively with the numbers
[n-1]\coloneqq\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
. Recording the edges in order of increasing edge-label—where the edge between vertex
i
and vertex
j
is recorded as the transposition
(i,j)
—gives a bijection between vertex- and edge-labeled trees and factorizations of
all

(n-1)!
long cycles in
S_{n}
into
(n-1)
transpositions. The tricky bit is to to identify which vertex- and edge-labeled trees have image in the original set
\textsc{fact}_{n}
(the answer relies on a certain embedding).

In this paper, we consider related problems in the
affine
symmetric group
\widetilde{S}_{n}
.

1.2.
The affine symmetric group

The
affine symmetric group

\widetilde{S}_{n}
can be thought of as the group of bijections
\widetilde{w}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
such that
[
BB05
, Chapter 8]

(1.1)

\widetilde{w}(i+n)=\widetilde{w}(i)+n\text{ and }\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{w}(i)=\binom{n+1}{2}.

Recall that the reflections of
\widetilde{S}_{n}
swap
i
and
j
for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
with
i\neq j\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
, and are written

(\!(i,j)\!).

We will be interested in certain factorizations into reflections of the element
\lambda_{n}\in\widetilde{S}_{n}
that acts on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by the translation

\lambda_{n}:x\mapsto x+(1,1,\ldots,1,-n+1).

Then
\lambda_{n}
can be expressed as a product of
2n-2
reflections (and not fewer). This
\lambda_{n}
will play the role of the long cycle.

1.3.
Trees

A reflection factorization for
\lambda_{n}

\mathsf{r}=\big[(\!(a_{0},b_{1})\!),(\!(a_{1},b_{2})\!),\ldots,(\!(a_{2n-3},b_{2n-2})\!)\big]

is called
tree-like
if
a_{k-1}<b_{k}
and
a_{k}=b_{k}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
.
We write
\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
for the set of all tree-like factorizations of
\lambda_{n}
.

Theorem
3.10
.

There is a bijection between
\underline{\textsc{tree}}_{n}
and
\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
, where
\underline{\textsc{tree}}_{n}
is the set of plane-embedded vertex-labeled trees on
[n]
with a marked edge adjacent to the vertex
n
(up to orientation preserving homeomorphism of the plane).

Corollary 1.1
.

For all
n\geq 2
, the number of tree-like factorizations of
\lambda_{n}
is

\left|\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}\right|=n!\mathrm{Cat}(n),

where
\mathrm{Cat}(n)=\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}
is the
n
th
Catalan number
.

1.4.
Cyclic trees

For each vertex-labeled tree, we will specify a preferred
cyclic
embedding in the plane.
Given a vertex-labeled tree
T\in\textsc{tree}_{n}
, its
cyclic
embedding is given as follows: draw
T
so that for every vertex
i\in[n]
its neighboring vertices
j
increase clockwise—with the exception that for
i\neq n
,
i
’s neighbor on the unique path from the vertex
n
to
i
is read as the central label
i
. The marked edge is the edge from
n
to its smallest neighbor. An example of a cyclically-embedded tree is given in
Figure

1
.

The restriction of
Corollary

3.12
to cyclically-embedded trees gives the notion of cyclic factorizations
\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
.

1.5.
Subwords

The factorizations in our version of the problem appear as labelings of certain Deodhar components for a braid variety constructed from an affine Kac-Moody group of type
A_{n-1}
. Write
\textsc{sub}_{n}
for the set of
maximal distinguished subwords
of the word

\bm{\lambda}_{n}\coloneqq[s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}]^{n-1}.

For simplicity in the introduction, we define
\textsc{sub}_{n}
to be the set of subwords with
2n-2
skips whose product is the identity (the equivalence with the usual definition is proven in
Corollary

5.15
). An example of an element of
\textsc{sub}_{n}
is given in
Figure

1
.

1.6.
Subwords and cyclic trees

Our main theorem is a bijection between
\textsc{sub}_{n}
and (cyclically-embedded) trees.

Theorem
6.1
.

There is a bijection between
\textsc{sub}_{n}
and
\textsc{tree}_{n}
.

1.7.
Enumeration

In previous work,
\textsc{sub}_{n}
was counted by Galashin, Lam, and Williams using braid varieties, a trace formula in the affine Hecke algebra due to Opdam, and an identity due to Haglund
[
Wil23
]
.

Theorem
7.4

(P. Galashin, T. Lam, N. Williams)
.

\left|R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|=(q-1)^{2n-2}[n]_{q}^{n-2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt|\textsc{sub}_{n}|=n^{n-2}.

Theorems

6.1
and
7.4
together give an elaborate new proof for the enumeration of
\textsc{tree}_{n}
.

Corollary 1.1

(Cayley’s formula)
.

|\textsc{tree}_{n}|=n^{n-2}.

Remark 1.1
.

Since the maximal distinguished subwords
\textsc{sub}_{n}
are naturally in bijection with trees, it makes sense to consider the braid variety
R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
to be a
q
-analogue of vertex-labeled trees. Compare with
[
Lei21
]
, which gives a very different
q
-analogue as the number of nilpotent linear operators on
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
.

The remainder of this paper has the same structure as the introduction, with a final section on future work.

, with takes in green, and skips in white and purple (decorated by the corresponding skip reflection, with the convention that
\overline{i}\coloneqq i-n
).

2.
The affine symmetric group

The
affine symmetric group

\widetilde{S}_{n}
can be thought of as the group of bijections
\widetilde{w}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
such that
[
BB05
, Chapter 8]

(2.1)

\widetilde{w}(i+n)=\widetilde{w}(i)+n\text{ and }\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{w}(i)=\binom{n+1}{2}.

We write
(\!(i,j)\!)
for the
affine reflection
that interchanges
i
and
j
(simultaneously interchanging
i+kn
and
j+kn
for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
); thus,
(\!(i,j)\!)=(\!(i+kn,j+kn)\!)
. We denote by
s_{j}\coloneqq(\!(j,j+1)\!)
the
simple reflections
. For clarity of typesetting, we use the notation
\overline{i}\coloneqq i-n
. We write
i\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
for the representative between
1
and
n
equal to
i
modulo
n
.

The
reflection length
of
w\in\widetilde{S}_{n}
is the minimal number of reflections required to express
w
as a product of reflections.

Proposition 2.1
.

For
0\leq i<n
,
s_{i}=(\!(i,i+1)\!)
is a right descent of
w\in\widetilde{S}_{n}
iff
w(i)>w(i+1)
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\lambda_{n}
be the translation that acts on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\lambda_{n}:x\mapsto x+(1,1,\ldots,1,-n+1).

Then
\lambda_{n}
is an element of
\widetilde{S}_{n}
with:

•

reduced word in simple reflections

(s_{0}s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1})^{n-1}
;

•

one-line notation

\big[n+1,n+2,\ldots,2n-1,-n(n-2)\big];\text{ and}

•

cycle notation

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(\ldots,i-n,i,i+n,\ldots\right)\right)\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\left(\ldots,nm+n(n-1),nm,nm-n(n-1),\ldots\right)\right).

Furthermore,
\lambda_{n}
has reflection length
2n-2
.

Proof.

The three descriptions are simple computations. The reflection length is easily deduced from
[
MP11
, Proposition 4.3]
(see also
[
LMPS19
, Theorem 4.25]
).∎

From its cycle decomposition, we see that
\lambda_{n}
acts on the integers as follows: it sends
k=0\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
to
k-n(n-1)
, and it sends
k\neq 0\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
to
k+n
.

3.
Trees

In
Section

3.1
, we describe sets of certain
tree-like
factorizations of
\lambda_{n}
in the affine symmetric group, which we will show in
Section

3.2
are encoded by clockwise walks around embedded vertex-labeled trees.

Given a finite sequence of reflections

\mathsf{r}=\left[(\!(a_{0},b_{1})\!),(\!(a_{1},b_{2})\!),\ldots\right]
and
k\in[n]
, write
r_{\ell}=(\!(a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell})\!)
for the
\ell
th reflection in the sequence. We say that
\mathsf{r}
is a
factorization
of
w\in\widetilde{S}_{n}
if
w=\prod_{i}(\!(a_{i},b_{i+1})\!)
; it is of
minimal length
if the number of reflections is equal to the reflection length of
w
. For
w\in\widetilde{S}_{n}
, write
\textsc{fact}(w)
for the set of its minimal length reflection factorizations.

3.1.
Tree-like factorizations

Definition 3.1
.

A minimal length reflection factorization

\mathsf{r}=\big[(\!(a_{0},b_{1})\!),(\!(a_{1},b_{2})\!),\ldots,(\!(a_{2n-3},b_{2n-2})\!)\big]\in\textsc{fact}(\lambda_{n})

is
tree-like
if
a_{k-1}<b_{k}
and
a_{k}=b_{k}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
.
We write
\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
for the set of all tree-like factorizations of
\lambda_{n}
.

Example 3.2
.

For
n=10
, the following factorization (see
Figure

1
) is tree-like:

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}=\Big[

\displaystyle(\!(\overline{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{.8}{10}}}2)\!),(\!(2\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{.8}{10}})\!),(\!(\overline{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{.8}{10}}}5)\!),(\!(57)\!),(\!(\overline{7}5)\!),(\!(\overline{5}1)\!),(\!(13)\!),(\!(\overline{3}1)\!),(\!(14)\!),

\displaystyle(\!(\overline{4}1)\!),(\!(18)\!),(\!(\overline{8}6)\!),(\!(68)\!),(\!(\overline{8}1)\!),(\!(15)\!),(\!(5\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{.8}{10}})\!),(\!(\overline{\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{.8}{10}}}9)\!),(\!(9\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{.8}{10}})\!)\Big].

We say that a reflection
r_{\ell}

increases
an integer
k
if

r_{\ell}r_{\ell+1}\cdots r_{2n-2}(k)>r_{\ell+1}\cdots r_{2n-2}(k),

and we say
r_{\ell}

decreases

k
if

r_{\ell}r_{\ell+1}\cdots r_{2n-2}(k)<r_{\ell+1}\cdots r_{2n-2}(k).

Since each
r_{\ell}
is a reflection, there exist unique distinct integers
a,b\in[n]
such that
r_{\ell}
increases
a
and decreases
b
. For

\mathsf{r}=\big[(\!(a_{0},b_{1})\!),(\!(a_{1},b_{2})\!),\ldots,(\!(a_{2n-3},b_{2n-2})\!)\big]\in\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n},

write

(3.1)

\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{r},k)=[b_{i_{1}}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n,\ldots,b_{i_{\ell}}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n]

for the sequence of
b_{i_{j}}
(modulo
n
) for which
a_{i_{j}-1}=k\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
(the abbreviation is for
neighbors
).

Example 3.3
.

If we track the progression of the integer
0
in
Example

3.2
as the list of products
r_{\ell}r_{\ell+1}\cdots r_{2n-2}(0)
for
\ell=1,\ldots,2n-2
, we obtain the sequence

\displaystyle\big[

\displaystyle-90,-88,-80,-75,-73,-65,-59,-57,-49,

\displaystyle-46,-39,-32,-24,-22,-19,-15,-10,-1\big].

Note that every reflection decreases
0
. On the other hand,
1
is unchanged until the
15
th reflection
(\!(15)\!)
, which increases
1
to
5
, and is next modified by the
6
th reflection
(\!(\overline{5}1)\!)
, which increases
5
to
11
. Observe that exactly two reflections increase
1
.

We make the observations of
Example

3.3
precise in the following proposition,
which gives a condition on factorizations equivalent to being tree-like. This equivalent condition will be easier to check on the factorizations arising from distinguished subwords in
Section

5
.

Proposition 3.4
.

A factorization
\mathsf{r}=\left[r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{2n-2}\right]\in\textsc{fact}(\lambda_{n})
is tree-like if and only if there exist
a_{0},\dots,a_{2n-2}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(3.2)

r_{\ell}=(\!(a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})\!)\text{ and }|a_{\ell}-a_{\ell-1}|<n\text{ for }\ell=1,\dots,2n-2.

Proof.

First suppose that
\mathsf{r}\in\textsc{fact}(\lambda_{n})
is tree-like. Then we can choose

a_{0}<\cdots<a_{2n-2}

such that
r_{\ell}=(\!(a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})\!)
. Since for any
1\leq\ell<2n-2

r_{\ell}\cdots r_{2n-2}(a_{2n-2})=a_{\ell-1}<a_{2n-2},

it follows that every
r_{\ell}
decreases
a_{2n-2}
. So we must have
a_{0}=a_{2n-2}=0\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
because the only integers that
\lambda_{n}
decreases are the multiples of
n
.

Now since
\lambda_{n}
maps each
k\in[n-1]
to
k+n
, there must exist at least two
r_{\ell}
which either increase or decrease
k
. But each
r_{\ell}
decreases
n
and increases some
k\neq n
, so by a pigeonhole argument, there are in fact exactly two unique factors
r_{\ell}
for each
k\neq n
which increase
k
. If
r_{i}
and
r_{j}
are
the
two factors which increase
k
, then

k+n=r_{1}\cdots r_{2n-2}(k)=k+(a_{i}-a_{i-1})+(a_{j}-a_{j-1}).

So we must have
a_{\ell}-a_{\ell-1}<n
for all
\ell
.

For the other direction, fix
\mathsf{r}\in\textsc{fact}(\lambda_{n})
satisfying
Equation

3.2
.
Note that the second condition
|a_{\ell}-a_{\ell-1}|<n
implies that for a given
k\neq n
there must exist
1\leq i<j\leq 2n-2
such that
r_{i}
and
r_{j}
increase
k
. Since we only have
2n-2
factors, it follows again from a pigeonhole argument that each factor
must
increase some
k\neq n
, and for each
k\neq n
, there are exactly two
r_{\ell}
which increase
k
.

Now, since
0
is sent to
-n(n-1)
and each
r_{\ell}
can only decrease
0
by at most
n-1
, we have that
0
needs to be decreased by at least
n
of the
r_{\ell}
. Note that
a_{2n-2}
is either increased or decreased by every
r_{\ell}
. If
a_{2n-2}\neq 0\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
, then
a_{2n-2}
is increased by exactly two of the
r_{\ell}
and decreased by all the others. Since each
r_{\ell}
decreases only one integer
\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
, it follows that the two factors which increase
a_{2n-2}
are the only factors that can decrease
n
. So we must have
n=2
. But in that case, there is only one minimal length reflection factorization of
\lambda_{2}
satisfying
Equation

3.2
:

(\!(0,1)\!)(\!(1,2)\!)=(\!(3,2)\!)(\!(2,1)\!).

This factorization is tree-like, so the equivalence also holds for
n=2
.

For
n>2
, it follows that
a_{2n-2}=0\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
. Since none of the
r_{\ell}
increase
0
(since every
r_{\ell}
increases a
k\in[n-1]
), it follows that all
r_{\ell}
must decrease
0
. This implies that

a_{0}<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{2n-2},

so that
\mathsf{r}
is tree-like.
∎

Since every reflection
r_{\ell}
must decrease
0
, we immediately obtain the following corollaries.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\mathsf{r}\in\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
with
r_{\ell}=(\!(a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})\!)
. Then
a_{0}=a_{2n-2}=0\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
.

Corollary 3.6
.

For
k\in[n-1]
, there are exactly two reflections that increase
k
—the first and last reflections to use a number equal to
k\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
. We denote these reflections

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}^{k}_{1}=(\!(\overline{b}_{k},k)\!)

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathsf{r}^{k}_{2}=(\!(k,b_{k})\!)\text{ if }b_{k}>k\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}^{k}_{1}=(\!(b_{k},k)\!)

\displaystyle\text{ and }\mathsf{r}^{k}_{2}=(\!(\overline{k},b_{k})\!)\text{ if }b_{k}<k.

We call
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{1}
the
left end
of its pair and
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{2}
the
right end
of its pair.

Proof.

In the proof of
Proposition

3.4
, we observed that for each
k\neq n
, there are unique factors
r_{i}
and
r_{j}
, where
i<j
, which increase
k
, and every
r_{\ell}
is in one of these pairs. Assume
b_{k}>k
. Then
r_{\ell}\cdots r_{2n-2}(k)=k
for
\ell>j
, so
r_{j}=(\!(k,b_{k})\!)
for some
b_{k}>k
. Moreover,

r_{\ell}\cdots r_{2n-2}(k)=\begin{cases}b_{k}&\text{if }i<\ell\leq j,\\
k+n&\text{if }\ell\leq i,\end{cases}

so
r_{i}=(\!(b_{k},k+n)\!)=(\!(\overline{b}_{k},k)\!)
. The case for
b_{k}<k
is similar.
∎

3.2.
Tree embeddings

Definition 3.7
.

We write
\underline{\textsc{tree}}_{n}
for the set of plane-embedded vertex-labeled trees on
[n]
with a marked edge adjacent to the vertex
n
(up to orientation preserving homeomorphism of the plane).

Example 3.8
.

The
30=3!\cdot\mathrm{Cat}(3)
trees in
\underline{\textsc{tree}}_{4}
are illustrated in
Figure

2
.

4

4

4

4

4
1
3
2

Figure 2.
The
30=3!\cdot\mathrm{Cat}(3)
trees in
\underline{\textsc{tree}}_{4}
. Each tree has only the vertex
4
labeled, and so corresponds to
3!
vertex-labeled trees in
\underline{\textsc{tree}}_{4}
by choosing a labeling of the unlabeled vertices by
1,2,3
.

Given an embedded vertex-labeled tree
T\in\underline{\textsc{tree}}_{n}
, we produce a sequence
\mathsf{r}^{T}
of
2n-2
affine reflections (this sequence will turn out to be a tree-like factorization of
\lambda_{n}
). Starting at the vertex labeled
n
, walk around the embedded tree
T
clockwise (so that every edge is traversed exactly twice)—initially walking along the marked edge adjacent to
n
. Record the edges visited as

(3.3)

\Big[(v_{0},v_{1}),(v_{1},v_{2}),\ldots,(v_{2n-3},v_{2n-2})\Big]

with the convention that
v_{0}=n
and
v_{2n-2}=n
. Then
\mathsf{r}^{T}
is defined to be the following sequence of
2n-2
affine reflections:

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}^{T}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big[(\!(i_{1},j_{1})\!),(\!(i_{2},j_{2})\!),\ldots,(\!(i_{2(n-1)},j_{2(n-1)})\!)\Big],\text{ where}

\displaystyle(\!(i_{k},j_{k})\!)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}(\!(v_{k-1},v_{k})\!)&\text{if }v_{k-1}<v_{k}\\
(\!(\overline{v}_{k-1},v_{k})\!)&\text{if }v_{k-1}>v_{k}\end{cases}\text{ and }\overline{i}=i-n.

Conversely, given
\mathsf{r}\in\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
, we define a corresponding plane embedded tree
T^{\mathsf{r}}
.
T^{\mathsf{r}}
has vertex set
[n]
; for each reflection
\mathsf{r}_{i}
that can be written as
(\!(a,b)\!)
with
1\leq a<b\leq n
, there is an edge connecting
a
and
b
(exactly half the reflections have this property by
Corollary

3.6
). It follows from
Definition

4.1
that
T^{\mathsf{r}}
is connected, and so it must be a tree since it has only
n-1
edges. The embedding of
T^{\mathsf{r}}
is determined by placing the neighbors of vertex
k
clockwise around
k
in the order in which they appear in
\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{r},k)
.

Example 3.9
.

The factorization
\mathsf{r}
in
Example

3.2
corresponds to the embedded tree
T
in
Figure

1
, where the marked edge adjacent to
10
is
(10,2)
.

Theorem 3.10
.

For any embedded vertex-labeled tree
T\in\underline{\textsc{tree}}_{n}
,
\mathsf{r}^{T}
is a tree-like factorization. For any tree-like factorization
\mathsf{r}\in\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
,
T^{\mathsf{r}}
is an embedded vertex-labeled tree. The maps
T\mapsto\mathsf{r}^{T}
and
\mathsf{r}\mapsto T^{\mathsf{r}}
are mutually inverse bijections between
\underline{\textsc{tree}}_{n}
and
\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathsf{r}^{T}
is a factorization in
\textsc{fact}(\lambda_{n})
. For
1\leq i\leq n-1
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we wish to show that
i+mn
is sent to
i+(m+1)n
to conclude that the composition of reflections produced by
\mathsf{r}^{T}
gives the first product of cycles in
Proposition

2.2
. By periodicity, it is enough to show this for
m=0
.

We will compute the composition of the reflections from right to left and show that we obtain
\lambda_{n}
. We record the list of reflections by starting at the vertex labeled
n
and walking around the tree
T
counterclockwise, initially walking along the marked edge incident with
n
. This allows us to read the list of vertices in
Equation

3.3
from right to left. Suppose the first edge using the vertex
i
encountered on this counterclockwise walk is the edge
(j,i)
traversed from the vertex
j
to
i
.

•

If
i<j
, then we record the reflection
(\!(i,j)\!)
, which sends
i
to
j
—and all edges encountered until we revisit the edge
(i,j)
(now traversed from the vertex
i
to
j
) do not involve
j
. The second time the edge is revisited, we record the reflection
(\!(j-n,i)\!)=(\!(j,i+n)\!)
, and thus sends
j
to
i+n
.

•

If
i>j
, then we record the reflection
(\!(i-n,j)\!)
, which sends
i
to
j+n
. Until we walk on this edge again, all other edges will not affect
j
. The second time the edge is revisited, we record the reflection
(\!(j,i)\!)=(\!(j+n,i+n)\!)
, which sends
j+n
to
i+n
.

In each case, we conclude that
i
is sent to
i+n
.

It remains to show that
\mathsf{r}^{T}
also gives the second product of cycles in
Proposition

2.2
—again by periodicity, it is enough to show
n
is sent to
n-n(n-1)
. Since for every
1\leq i<j\leq n
every edge
(i,j)
is traversed twice, once as just
(i,j)
and once as
(j-n,i)
, and since every pair of adjacent reflections share a letter,
n
is subtracted from the quantity exactly
(n-1)
times—once for each pair of edges on the walk. Thus,
n
is sent to
n-n(n-1)
, as desired.

By construction, the factorization
\mathsf{r}^{T}
of
\lambda_{n}
satisfies
Definition

3.1
, and so is tree-like. It is clear that the inverse is given by the map
\mathsf{r}\mapsto T^{\mathsf{r}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
\mathsf{r}=\left[r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{2n-2}\right]\in\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
, and write
r_{\ell}=(\!(a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})\!)
with

a_{0}<\cdots<a_{2n-2}
. Then

(i)

If
(\!(a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})\!)
is to the left of
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{1}
or to the right of
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{2}
, then
a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell}\not=k\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
.

(ii)

If
(\!(a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})\!)
is between
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{1}
and
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{2}
, then
a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell}\not=b_{k}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
.

(iii)

(\!(a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})\!)
is between
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{1}
and
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{2}
iff
(\!(\overline{a}_{\ell},a_{\ell-1})\!)
is also between
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{1}
and
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{2}
.

3.3.
Enumeration

The bijection of
Theorem

3.10
gives the following interesting enumeration for the tree-like factorizations of
\lambda_{n}
.

Corollary 3.12
.

For all
n\geq 2
, the number of tree-like factorizations of
\lambda_{n}
is

\left|\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}\right|=n!\mathrm{Cat}(n),

where
\mathrm{Cat}(n)=\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}
is the
n
th
Catalan number
.

Proof.

The number of rooted planar trees with
n
vertices is
\mathrm{Cat}(n)
. By marking an edge, we remove any symmetries. Since the number of plane-embedded vertex-labeled trees with
n
vertices is
n!\mathrm{Cat}(n)
, we conclude the same enumeration for
\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
by
Theorem

3.10
.
∎

Remark 3.13
.

Corollary

3.12
is not our titular “elaborate proof”—we are relying on previous combinatorial enumerations of rooted planar trees. The issue is that we do not know what braid varieties over the loop group for
\mathrm{SL}_{n}
correspond to tree-like factorizations; Minh-Tâm Trinh has constructed certain “generalized Steinberg varieties” using unipotent elements that give this enumeration—but using the simple Lie group and not its loop group.

4.
Cyclic trees

In
Section

4.1
, we describe sets of certain
cyclic
factorizations of
\lambda_{n}
in the affine symmetric group, which we will show in
Section

4.2
are encoded by clockwise walks around
cyclically-embedded
vertex-labeled trees.

4.1.
Cyclic factorizations

Definition 4.1
.

A tree-like factorization

\mathsf{r}=\big[(\!(a_{0},b_{1})\!),(\!(a_{1},b_{2})\!),\ldots,(\!(a_{2n-3},b_{2n-2})\!)\big]\in\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}

is
cyclic
if

(i)

if
\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{r},n)=[b_{i_{1}},\ldots,b_{i_{\ell}}]
then

b_{i_{1}}<\cdots<b_{i_{\ell}};\text{ and}

(ii)

for any
1\leq k<n
, if
\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{r},k)=[b_{i_{1}},\ldots,b_{i_{\ell}}]
, then there exists some
1\leq j\leq\ell
for which

b_{i_{j}}<b_{i_{j+1}}<\cdots<b_{i_{\ell-1}}<k<b_{i_{1}}<\cdots<b_{i_{j-1}}.

We write
\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
for the set of all cyclic factorizations of
\lambda_{n}
.

Note that
b_{i_{\ell}}
is replaced by
k
in
Definition

def:cyclic_factorizations
(ii)
.

Example 4.2
.

The tree-like factorization in
Example

3.2
is also cyclic.
Definition

def:cyclic_factorizations
(i)
is satisfied bececause
\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{r},10)=[2,5,9]
and
2<5<9
. As an example of
Definition

def:cyclic_factorizations
(ii)
,
\mathrm{nb}(\mathsf{r},1)=[3,4,8,5]
satisfies
1<3<4<8
.

Our goal now is to give an equivalent characterization of cyclic factorizations, again to more easily connect with the factorizations arising from trees in
Section

4.2
. We will require the following easy lemma concerning cyclic orderings.

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose that
a,v,b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
v-n<a<v<b<v+n
. Let
1\leq\widetilde{a},\widetilde{v},\widetilde{b}\leq n
be the corresponding values modulo
n
. Then
a+n<b
if and only if
\widetilde{a}<\widetilde{b}<\widetilde{v}
,
\widetilde{v}<\widetilde{a}<\widetilde{b}
, or
\widetilde{b}<\widetilde{v}<\widetilde{a}
.

Proof.

Both statements are obviously equivalent to

v-n<a<b-n<v<a+n<b<v+n.\qed

The following proposition will be used to connect cyclic factorizations with distinguished subwords in
Section

6
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{r}\in\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
. By
Corollary

3.5
, we can write

(r_{1}\cdots r_{j-1})r_{j}(r_{j-1}\cdots r_{1})=(\!(0,m_{j})\!),

for each
j=1,\dots,2n-2
. Then
\mathsf{r}
is cyclic if and only if

m_{1}<\cdots<m_{2n-2}.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathsf{r}
is a cyclic factorization, and fix
1\leq j<2n-2
. Write
r_{j}=(\!(a,v)\!)
and
r_{j+1}=(\!(v,b)\!)
with
a<v<b
. By adding a multiple of
n
if necessary, we can assume that
r_{1}\cdots r_{j-1}(a)=0
. There are four cases to consider
1
1

1

These four cases correspond to the four cases in
Figure

6
and in
Section

6.2
.
, all of which will be handled using
Corollary

3.11
:

(a)

Suppose
r_{j}
and
r_{j+1}
are both the left ends of their pairs. Then
m_{j}=v<b=m_{j+1}
.

(b)

Suppose
r_{j}
is the right end of its pair and
r_{j+1}
is the left end of its pair. Then
m_{j}=a+n
and
m_{\ell+1}=b
. By
Proposition

3.4
we also know that
v-n<a<v<b<v+n
. It then follows from
Definition

def:cyclic_factorizations
(ii)
and
Lemma

4.3
that
m_{j}<m_{j+1}
.

(c)

Suppose
r_{j}
and
r_{j+1}
are both the right ends of their pairs. Then
m_{j}=a+n<v+n=m_{j+1}
.

(d)

Suppose
r_{j}
is the left end of its pair and
r_{j+1}
is the right end of its pair. Then these must be the same pair, so
a=b-n
, and
m_{j}=v<v+n=m_{j+1}
.

Suppose now that we have a tree-like factorization with
m_{1}<\cdots<m_{2n-2}
. We begin by considering
Definition

def:cyclic_factorizations
(i)
. It follows from
Corollary

3.11
that we can write
r_{1},\dots,r_{2n-2}
as

(\!(\bar{n},v_{1})\!),\dots,(\!(v_{1},n)\!),(\!(\bar{n},v_{2})\!),\dots,(\!(v_{2},n)\!),\dots,(\!(\bar{n},v_{\ell})\!),\dots,(\!(v_{\ell},n)\!).

Consider the adjacent factors
r_{j}=(\!(v_{i},n)\!)
and
r_{j+1}=(\!(\bar{n},v_{i+1})\!)
. We have
v_{i+1}-v_{i}=m_{j+1}-m_{j}>0
, so
v_{i}<v_{i+1}
.

Now consider
Definition

def:cyclic_factorizations
(ii)
. It follows from
Corollary

3.11
that we can write
r_{1},\dots,r_{2n-2}
as

\dots,(\!(\bar{a}_{\ell},k)\!),(\!(k,a_{1})\!),\dots,(\!(\bar{a}_{1},k)\!),\dots,(\!(k,a_{\ell-1})\!),\dots,(\!(\bar{a}_{\ell-1},k)\!),(\!(k,a_{\ell})\!),\dots,

where each
a_{i}=v_{i}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
. Consider the adjacent factors
r_{j}=(\!(\bar{a}_{i},k)\!)
and
r_{j+1}=(\!(k,a_{i+1})\!)
for
1\leq i\leq\ell-2
. We have
a_{i+1}-a_{i}=m_{j+1}-m_{j}>0
, so
a_{i}<a_{i+1}
. We also have
k-n<a_{i}-n<k<a_{i+1}<k+n
, so by
Lemma

4.3
either
v_{i}<v_{i+1}<k
,
k<v_{i}<v_{i+1}
, or
v_{i+1}<k<v_{i}
.
Definition

def:cyclic_factorizations
(ii)
follows, so that
\mathsf{r}
is cyclic.
∎

4.2.
Cyclic embeddings

Definition 4.5
.

We write
\textsc{tree}_{n}
for the set of vertex-labeled trees (as abstract graphs).

For each vertex-labeled tree, we will now specify a preferred
cyclic
embedding in the plane. (We note that there is some similarity with
[
GY02
, Section 3]
.)

Definition 4.6
.

Given a vertex-labeled tree
T\in\textsc{tree}_{n}
, its
cyclic
embedding is given as follows: draw
T
so that for every vertex
i\in[n]
its neighboring vertices
j
increase clockwise—with the exception that for
i\neq n
,
i
’s neighbor on the unique path from the vertex
n
to
i
is read as the central label
i
. The marked edge is the edge from
n
to its smallest neighbor.

To make the clockwise increasing condition easy to see in examples, we direct each edge in
T
towards the vertex
n
.

Example 4.7
.

All 16 trees in
\textsc{tree}_{4}
are drawn in
Figure

3
in their cyclic embedding; a larger example is given in
Figure

1
.

&(\!(\overline{4}1)\!)(\!(14)\!)(\!(\overline{4}2)\!)(\!(24)\!)(\!(\overline{4}3)\!)(\!(34)\!)\end{array}

Figure 3.
The set
\textsc{tree}_{4}
, the 16 vertex-labeled trees on
4
vertices, cyclically embedded in the plane according to
Section

4.2
. Below each tree is the corresponding cyclic factorization of
\lambda_{4}

.

By construction,
Theorem

3.10
restricts from all tree-like factorizations and all embeddings to cyclic factorizations and embeddings.

Theorem 4.8
.

For any cyclically-embedded vertex-labeled tree
T\in\textsc{tree}_{n}
,
\mathsf{r}^{T}
is a cyclic factorization. For any cyclic factorization
\mathsf{r}\in\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
,
T^{\mathsf{r}}
is a cyclically-embedded vertex-labeled tree. The maps
T\mapsto\mathsf{r}^{T}
and
\mathsf{r}\mapsto T^{\mathsf{r}}
are mutually inverse bijections between
\textsc{tree}_{n}
and
\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
.

Remark 4.9
.

At this point we could use the known enumeration of
\textsc{tree}_{n}
to conclude that
|\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}|=n^{n-2}
. We will instead connect
\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
to certain maximal distinguished subwords in
Section

5
, connect these subwords to certain braid varieties in
Section

7.1
, use representation-theoretic methods to compute the point count of the braid varieties over a finite field with
q
elements, and then recover the cardinality of
\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
by sending
q\to 1
.

Remark 4.10
.

In analogy with the usual problem of minimal reflection factorizations of the long cycle in
S_{n}
and the noncrossing partition lattice, it seems natural to define a partial order on the prefixes of cyclic factorizations in
\widetilde{S}_{n}
. Unfortunately, for
n\geq 4
there are maximal chains in this partial order that no longer correspond to cyclic factorizations.

5.
Subwords

A
subword

\mathsf{u}
of a sequence
[s_{i_{1}},s_{i_{2}},\ldots,s_{i_{m}}]
of simple generators of the affine symmetric group
\widetilde{S}_{n}
(see
Section

2
for more details) is a sequence

\mathsf{u}=[u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{m}],\text{ where }u_{j}\in\{s_{i_{j}},e\}\text{ for all }j.

We call the letters
j
for which
u_{j}=e
,
skips
, and the letters
j
for which
u_{j}=s_{i_{j}}

takes
. For any such sequence, we set

(5.1)

\displaystyle u_{(j)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{j}\in\widetilde{S}_{n},\text{ and}

\displaystyle u^{(j)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq u_{j}\cdots u_{m}\in\widetilde{S}_{n}.

We say
\mathsf{u}
is a
w
-subword
if
u_{(m)}=w
.

5.1.
Maximal distinguished subwords

Definition 5.1
.

Write
\textsc{sub}_{n}
for the set of
maximal distinguished subwords
of the word

\bm{\lambda}_{n}\coloneqq[s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}]^{n-1}.

That is,
\textsc{sub}_{n}
is the set of subwords with
2n-2
skips whose product is the identity.

The
n
consecutive factors of length
n-1
of the word
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
—from the
i(n-1)
st letter to the
((i+1)(n-1)-1)
st letter—will be called
rows
. Drawing
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
with subsequence rows vertically aligned gives the notion of
columns
. We will typically depicting
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
or a subword
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
using an
n\times(n-1)
array.

We will show in
Corollary

5.15
that for this special case of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
,
Definition

5.1
recovers the usual notion of
distinguished

[
Deo85
]
.

Example 5.2
.

The
16
maximal distinguished subwords in
\textsc{sub}_{4}
are given in
Figure

5
. A larger example is given in
Figure

1
. See also
Figure

4
.

In preparation to connect subwords to trees, we associate a reflection to each skip in a subword in
\textsc{sub}_{n}
.

Definition 5.3
.

For
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
, define

(5.2)

\textsc{inv}(\mathsf{u})\coloneqq\big[r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{n(n-1)}\big]

where
r_{k}=u_{(i_{k-1})}s_{i_{k}}u_{(i_{k-1})}^{-1}
(the notation
u_{(i)}
is defined in
Equation

5.1
). We write
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
for the subsequence of
\textsc{inv}(\mathsf{u})
obtained by restricting to the
skips
of
\mathsf{u}
—that is, restricted to the indices
j
for which
u_{j}=e
—and call the subsequence
skip reflections
.

Remark 5.4
.

We will show in
Section

6
that
\textsc{sub}_{n}
is in bijection with
\textsc{tree}_{n}
—the skip reflections will determine the edges of the corresponding tree.

.

illustrates
\textsc{inv}(\mathsf{u})

.

Figure 5.
The
16
distinguished subwords in
\textsc{sub}_{4}
, with letters chosen in the subword indicated in green, positive skips in white, and negative skips in purple (and replaced by the corresponding inversions). Compare with
Figure

3
.

For a subword
\mathsf{u}=[u_{1},\dots,u_{n(n-1)}]
of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
, we encode the pattern of skips in an
indicator word

\psi(\mathsf{u})=[\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n(n-1)}]
, where

\psi_{i}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }u_{i}=e,\\
1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We define the
rotation
of a subword, denoted
\operatorname{rot}(\mathsf{u})
as the subword with indicator word

\left[\psi(\mathsf{u})_{n(n-1)},\psi(\mathsf{u})_{1},\dots,\psi(\mathsf{u})_{n(n-1)-1}\right].

Explicitly, we can write
\operatorname{rot}(\mathsf{u})_{i}
in terms of how it acts on the integers via

\operatorname{rot}(\mathsf{u})_{i}=\alpha_{+}u_{i-1}\alpha_{-},

where
\alpha_{+}(m)=m+1
and
\alpha_{-}(m)=m-1
.

Lemma 5.6
.

The rotation of an
e
-subword
\mathsf{u}
of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
is an
e
-subword.

Proof.

Write
u^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\operatorname{rot}(\mathsf{u})_{i}
. Then

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{1}\cdots u^{\prime}_{n(n-1)}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{+}u_{n(n-1)}u_{1}\cdots u_{n(n-1)-1}\alpha_{-}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{+}u_{n(n-1)}(u_{1}\cdots u_{n(n-1)})u_{n(n-1)}\alpha_{-}=e.\qed

Corollary 5.7
.

The rotation of a maximal distinguished subword of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
is again a maximal distinguished subword.

Remark 5.8
.

It might be interesting to determine the orbit structure of
\textsc{sub}_{n}
under cyclic rotation.

5.2.
Skip reflections are tree-like

Our eventual goal is to show that if
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
, then
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
gives a cyclic factorization of
\lambda_{n}
. We begin by showing that
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
is tree-like (
Definition

3.1
).

Proposition 5.9
.

For
\mathsf{u}
a subword of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
, write
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}=\left[r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\right]
. Let
i_{j}
denote the index of the skip corresponding to
r_{j}
. Then for any
i_{j}\leq\ell<i_{j+1}
,

r_{1}\cdots r_{j}=(\bm{\lambda}_{n})_{(\ell)}u_{(\ell)}^{-1}.

Proof.

This is
[
GLTW22
, Proposition 4.7]
. The idea is to notice that

(\bm{\lambda}_{n})_{(\ell)}^{-1}r_{1}\cdots r_{j}=\mathsf{u}_{(\ell)}^{-1}

because the
r_{i}
cancel the corresponding skips from
(\bm{\lambda}_{n})_{(\ell)}^{-1}
.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

If
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
, then
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}\in\textsc{fact}(\lambda_{n})
.

We can understand how a subword
\mathsf{u}
acts on
k
by looking at
\textsc{inv}(\mathsf{u})
. The
j
th reflection
(\!(a,b)\!)
in
\textsc{inv}(\mathsf{u})
with
a
or
b
equal to
k
modulo
n
will be either:

•

increases
(
u^{(j)}(k)>u^{(j+1)}(k)
), which occur along columns

•

decreases
(
u^{(j)}(k)<u^{(j+1)}(k)
), which occur along rows

•

skips (
u^{(j)}(k)=u^{(j+1)}(k)
), which appear as corners.

Example 5.11
.

It is helpful for understanding the subsequent proofs to interpret the various data on subwords in terms of cyclic trees (although the bijection will not be formally proven until
Section

6
).

Consider the set of reflections
(\!(a,b)\!)
in
\textsc{inv}(\mathsf{u})
with
a
or
b
equal to
k
modulo
n
. This set, when highlighted on
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
drawn using
n
rows and
n-1
columns, record what is seen as one goes clockwise around the vertex
k
in the tree corresponding to
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
. The set forms a connected path heading down and right on a torus (if the path goes below the
n
th row, the path continues in the same column in the first row; if the path goes to the right of the
(n-1)
st column, the path continues one row below in the first column):

•

for
k\neq 0\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
, the path starts down from the topmost box in column
k
; if
k=0\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
, then then path starts right from the top left box;

•

skips of
\mathsf{u}
correspond to neighbors of
k
; at skips, the path switches between going down and going right;

•

neighbors
a<k
(resp.
a>k
) are recorded as corners
(\!(a,k)\!)
and
(\!(\overline{k},a)\!)
(resp. corners
(\!(\overline{a},k)\!)
and
(\!(k,a)\!)
) in the same column of the strip;

•

the vertical distance between the corners
(\!(a,k)\!)
and
(\!(\overline{k},a)\!)
(or
(\!(\overline{a},k)\!)
and
(\!(k,a)\!)
) is given by the number of vertices on the connected component containing
a
of the tree without vertex
k
(and the reflections that appear use those vertices and
k
itself); and

•

the horizontal distance between clockwise adjacent neighbors of
k
is given by the length of the
run-leaf
between those neighbors (defined in
Section

6.2
).

: starting at the corner

, there is a run-leaf with label 2 corresponding to a horizontal step of length 2, then the neighbor

(there is a single vertical step since

only has

.

Lemma 5.12
.

If
\mathsf{u}
is an
e
-subword of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
, then for all integers
k
and all
a,b
with
0\leq b-a<n(n-1)
,

\big|\left(u_{a}\cdots u_{b}\right)(k)-k\big|\leq n-2,

where the indices are taken modulo
n(n-1)
.

Proof.

Any segment
\left(u_{a}\cdots u_{b}\right)
and any
k
such that

\left(u_{a}\cdots u_{b}\right)(k)-k\geq n-1

can be converted to the segment
\left(u_{b+1}\cdots u_{a-1}\right)
and
k^{\prime}=\left(u_{a}\cdots u_{b}\right)(k)
with

\left(u_{b+1}\cdots u_{a-1}\right)(k^{\prime})-k^{\prime}\leq 1-n,

since

\left(u_{b+1}\cdots u_{a-1}\right)\big(\left(u_{a}\cdots u_{b}\right)(k)\big)-\left(u_{a}\cdots u_{b}\right)(k)=k-\left(u_{a}\cdots u_{b}\right)(k)\leq 1-n.

So it suffices to consider the case
\left(u_{a}\cdots u_{b}\right)(k)-k\leq 1-n
, so that the number of terms in
u_{a}\cdots u_{b}
that decrease
k
(by one, since each
u_{i}
is a simple reflection) is at least
n-1
. Moreover, we can take the index
b
to be
n(n-1)
by rotating the subword. The terms that decrease
k
correspond to indices
a\leq i_{n-1}<\cdots<i_{1}\leq n(n-1)
such that
u_{i_{\ell}}=s_{k-\ell}
. Notice that
i_{n-1}\leq(n-1)^{2}+1
.

Since
\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{a-1}u_{a}\cdots u_{b}\right)(k)=k
, there must exist at least
n-1
terms in
(u_{1}\cdots u_{a-1})
that increase
(u_{a}\cdots u_{b})(k)
, so we can find indices
1\leq j_{1}\leq\cdots\leq j_{n-1}\leq a-1
such that
u_{j_{\ell}}=s_{k-\ell}
. Each of these indices must be on its own row and not on the last two rows (because
j_{n-1}\leq i_{n-1}-n\leq(n-2)(n-1)
. So we reach a contradiction because
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
only has
n
rows.
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

If
\mathsf{u}
is an
e
-subword of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
and
(\!(a,b)\!)\in\textsc{inv}(\mathsf{u})
, then
|b-a|\leq n-1
.

Proof.

We need to show that

\left|\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j+1)-\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j)\right|\leq n-1

for all
j=0,\dots,n(n-1)-1
. Suppose not. Since each inversion is an affine reflection, it is not possible to have

|\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j+1)-\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j)|=n,

so suppose that

\left|\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j+1)-\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j)\right|\geq n+1.

By
Lemma

5.12
there are two cases to consider:

(1)

\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j+1)\leq j<j+1\leq\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j)

(2)

\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j)\leq j<j+1\leq\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j+1)

In the first case, there must exist an index
1<\ell<j
such that
u_{\ell}=s_{\ell-1}
and

\left(u_{\ell+1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j)=\ell-1\quad\text{and}\quad\left(u_{\ell+1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j+1)=\ell.

Choose the smallest such
\ell
. Then

\left|\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{\ell-1}\right)(\ell-1)-\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{\ell-1}(\ell)\right)\right|=\left|\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}(j+1)\right)-\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j)\right|\geq n+1,

and

\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{\ell-1}\right)(\ell-1)\leq\ell-1<\ell\leq\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(\ell).

So the first case reduces to the second. Let

c=j-\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j)\quad\text{and}\quad d=\left(u_{1}\cdots u_{j}\right)(j+1)-(j+1),

so
c+d\geq n
. Now there must be indices
1\leq i_{d}<i_{d-1}\cdots<i_{1}\leq j
such that
u_{i_{\ell}}=s_{j+\ell}
. Each of these indices must be on its own row. Moreover,
i_{1}
cannot be on the same row as
j
.

Now there must also be indices
j+1\leq k_{1}<\cdots<k_{c}\leq n(n-1)
such that
u_{k_{\ell}}=s_{j-\ell}
. Again, each of these indices must be its own row. Moreover,
k_{1}
cannot be on the same row as
j
. It follows that we need to use at least
c+d+1\geq n+1
rows, which is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 5.14
.

For
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
,
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
is a tree-like factorization in
\underline{\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}}_{n}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}=\left[r_{1},\dots,r_{2n-2}\right]
. If the skips corresponding to
r_{\ell}
and
r_{\ell+1}
are at indices
i
and
j
, respectively, then

u_{(j-1)}(j-1)=u_{(i-1)}es_{i}\cdots s_{j-2}(j-1)=u_{(i-1)}(i).

Since
r_{\ell}=(\!(u_{(i-1)}(i-1),u_{(i-1)}(i))\!)
and
r_{\ell+1}=(\!(u_{(j-1)}(j-1),u_{(j-1)}(j))\!)
, it follows that there exist
a_{0},\dots a_{2n-2}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
r_{\ell}=(\!(a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})\!)
. The result then follows from
Lemma

5.13
and
Proposition

3.4
.
∎

We now show that
Definition

5.1
captures the usual definition of
distinguished
subword, that a simple reflection must be used if it causes the current product to decrease in weak order.

Corollary 5.15
.

For
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
, if
u_{(j)}s_{j}<u_{(j)}
then
u_{j+1}=s_{j}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
u_{(j)}s_{j}<u_{(j)}
iff
u_{(j)}(j)>u_{(j)}(j+1)
. Suppose that
u_{j+1}=e
, so the inversion corresponding to this skip is
(\!(u_{(j)}(j),u_{(j)}(j+1))\!)
. By the proofs of
Lemma

5.14
and
Proposition

3.4
, it follows that
u_{(j)}(j)<u_{(j)}(j+1)
. The result follows by contraposition.
∎

5.3.
Skip reflections are cyclic

We continue to work towards the bijection between
\textsc{sub}_{n}
and
\textsc{tree}_{n}
by now showing that
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
is actually a cyclic factorization in
\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
.

Lemma 5.16
.

For
\mathsf{u}
a subword of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
, write
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}=\left[r_{1},\dots,r_{\ell}\right]
, and let
i_{j}
denote the index of the skip corresponding to
r_{j}
. Then

(r_{1}\cdots r_{j-1})r_{j}(r_{j-1}\cdots r_{1})=\left(\!\left(0,i_{j}+\left\lfloor\frac{i_{j}-1}{n-1}\right\rfloor\right)\!\right).

Proof.

From Lemma
5.9
, we have

\displaystyle(r_{1}\cdots r_{j-1})r_{j}(r_{j-1}\cdots r_{1})

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{n})_{(i_{j}-1)}u_{(i_{j}-1)}^{-1}(u_{(i_{j}-1)}s_{i_{j}-1}u_{(i_{j}-1)}^{-1})u_{(i_{j}-1)}(\lambda_{n})_{(i_{j}-1)}^{-1}

\displaystyle=(s_{0}\cdots s_{i_{j}-2})s_{i_{j}-1}(s_{i_{j}-2}\cdots s_{0})

\displaystyle=(\!(s_{0}\cdots s_{i_{j}-2}(i_{j}-1),s_{0}\cdots s_{i_{j}-2}(i_{j}))\!)

\displaystyle=(\!(0,s_{0}\cdots s_{i_{j}-2}(i_{j}))\!).

Write
a_{k}\coloneqq s_{0}\cdots s_{k-2}(k)
. If
k<n
, then
a_{k}=k
. Otherwise,

\displaystyle a_{k}

\displaystyle=s_{0}\cdots s_{k-n}(k)=s_{0}\cdots s_{k-n-1}(k+1)

\displaystyle=s_{0}\cdots s_{k-n-1}(k-n+1)+n=a_{k-(n-1)}+n.

This shows that
a_{k}=k+\lfloor(k-1)/(n-1)\rfloor
, so the result follows.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

For
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
,
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
is a cyclic factorization in
\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}=\left[r_{1},\dots,r_{2n-2}\right]
.
From
Lemma

5.14
, we know
\left[r_{1},\ldots,r_{2n-2}\right]
satisfies
Definition

3.1
, so it remains to show
Definition

def:cyclic_factorizations
(i)
and
Definition

def:cyclic_factorizations
(ii)
. These follow from
Lemma

5.16
and
Proposition

4.4
.
∎

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
\mathsf{r}\in\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
. Then there exists a subword
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
such that
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\left[r_{1},\ldots,r_{2n-2}\right]
is a cyclic factorization of
\lambda_{n}
. By
Corollary

3.11
, we can write
r_{\ell}=(\!(a_{\ell-1},a_{\ell})\!)
with
a_{\ell-1}<a_{\ell}
,
a_{0}=0
, and
a_{2n-2}=n(n-1)
.

As in
Proposition

4.4
, for
1\leq j\leq 2n-2
, define integers
m_{j}
by

(r_{1}\cdots r_{j-1})r_{j}(r_{j-1}\cdots r_{1})=(\!(0,m_{j})\!).

Then

m_{2n-2}=a_{2n-1}+n<a_{2n-2}+n=n(n-1)+n=n^{2}.

By
Proposition

4.4
, we have

0=a_{0}<a_{1}=m_{1}<m_{2}<\cdots<m_{2n-2}<n^{2}.

For
j=1,\dots,2n-2
, define

i_{j}\coloneqq m_{j}-\left\lfloor\frac{m_{j}-1}{n}\right\rfloor\text{ so that }m_{j}=i_{j}-\left\lfloor\frac{i_{j}-1}{n-1}\right\rfloor.

Notice that

1\leq i_{j}\leq n^{2}-1-\left\lfloor\frac{n^{2}-2}{n}\right\rfloor=n(n-1),

so we can take
\mathsf{u}
to be the subword of
\lambda_{n}
with skips at indices
i_{j}
. By
Lemma

5.16
, we have
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}=\left[r_{1},\dots,r_{2n-2}\right]
. Then, since
r_{1}\cdots r_{2n-2}=\lambda_{n}
, it follows from
Proposition

5.9
that
\mathsf{u}
is a distinguished subword.
∎

Corollary 5.19
.

The map
\mathsf{u}\mapsto\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
is a bijection between
\textsc{sub}_{n}
and
\textsc{f}\widetilde{\textsc{a}}\textsc{ct}_{n}
.

6.
Subwords and cyclic trees

In this section, we prove our main theorem—that there is a bijection between maximal distinguished subwords of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
and (cyclically-embedded) vertex-labeled trees with
n
vertices.

Theorem 6.1
.

There is a bijection between
\textsc{sub}_{n}
and
\textsc{tree}_{n}
.

The proof of
Theorem

6.1
will occupy the next two subsections.

6.1.
From subwords to cyclic trees

The forward direction of the bijection is easy: given
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
, compute the inversions of the skips
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
, then create a tree
T\in\textsc{tree}_{n}
with edges
(a,b)
between
a<b
when
(\!(a,b)\!)
and
(\!(\overline{b},a)\!)
appear as reflections in
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
. This tree can then be cyclically embedded using
Section

4.2
.

6.2.
From cyclic trees to subwords

The other direction of the bijection is a little more difficult. To more easily describe it, we decorate the trees with
run-leaves
.

Fix a cyclic tree
T\in\textsc{tree}_{n}
. To each vertex
v\neq n
we will attach
\deg(v)
many
run-leaves
, so that in a clockwise walk around
T
, edges and run-leaves alternate. At the vertex
n
, we instead add
\deg(n)+1
many leaves: two between the smallest and largest neighbors of
n
(because
T
is cyclically embedded, these vertices will be adjacent). We index the run-leaves based on when we see them in the clockwise walk starting from
n
towards its smallest neighbor, so that our walk visits run-leaves
l_{0},\ldots,l_{2n-2}
and edges
e_{0},\ldots,e_{2n-1}
in the order

(6.1)

\left[l_{0},e_{0},l_{1},e_{1},l_{2},\ldots,e_{2n-1},l_{2n-2}\right].

We now label each run-leaf
l_{k}
with an integer
\ell(l_{k})\coloneqq\ell_{k}
with
1\leq\ell_{k}\leq n-1
as follows (for now, ignore the first and last run-leaves,
l_{0}
and
l_{2n-2}
, attached to vertex
n
). The run-leaf
l_{k}
is situated between the two edges
e_{k-1}=(v_{k-1},v_{k})
and
e_{k}=(v_{k},v_{k+1})
, incident to the vertex
v_{k}
to which
l_{k}
has been attached. The label
\ell_{k}
is assigned according to the following four cases, illustrated in
Figure

6
:

\begin{array}[]{|lc|cr|}\hline\cr&&&\\[-4.30554pt]

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}&(d)\\
&v_{k}=v_{k-1}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}{\ell_{k}}}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ n-1)&{\color[rgb]{0,0.7,0}{\ell_{k}}}=n-1&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 6.
Run-leaf rules, where the dashed line denoting our walk around a cyclic tree
T
. The walk begins at vertex
n
and steps first towards the smallest neighbor of
n
walking clockwise.

(a)

if
v_{k+1}\neq v_{k-1}
and the path from
v_{k+1}
to
n
goes through
v_{k}
and
v_{k-1}
, then
\ell_{k}=v_{k+1}-v_{k}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n-1
.

(b)

if the paths from
v_{k-1}
to
n
and from
v_{k+1}
go through
v_{k}
, then
\ell_{k}=v_{k+1}-v_{k-1}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n-1
.

(c)

if
v_{k+1}\neq v_{k-1}
and the path from
v_{k-1}
to
n
goes through
v_{k}
and
v_{k+1}
, then
\ell_{k}=v_{k}-v_{k-1}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n-1
.

(d)

if
v_{k+1}=v_{k-1}
, then
\ell_{k}=n-1
.

We can view these four cases as specializations of the general rule for
1\leq\ell_{k}\leq n-1
:

\displaystyle\ell_{k}

\displaystyle=v^{\prime}_{k+1}-v^{\prime}_{k-1}\;\mathrm{mod}\;(n-1),\text{ where }

\displaystyle v^{\prime}_{k\pm 1}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{k}&\text{if }v_{k\pm 1}\text{ is on the path from }v_{k}\text{ to }n,\\
v_{k\pm 1}&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Finally, we define
\ell_{0}
to be
v_{1}
, the smallest neighbor of
n
, and
\ell_{2n-2}
to be
(n-1)-v_{2n-3}
; note that
v_{2n-3}
is necessarily the largest neighbor of
n
and
\ell_{2n-2}
is possibly zero. By construction, the sum of the labels of the run-leaves adjacent to any vertex
k\in[n]
is
n-1
.

Remark 6.2
.

Because there is a unique path from
v_{k}
to
n
, we never have the case
v_{k-1}
v_{k}
\ell_{k}
v_{k+1}
.

The subword
\mathsf{u}^{T}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
is now described using the sequence of run-leaves and edges in
Equation

6.1
to describe its takes and skips (see
Section

5
for these definitions): each run-leaf
l_{k}
corresonds to a series of
\ell_{k}-1
successive takes, while each edge
e_{k}
corresponds to a single skip.
Lemma

5.13
,
Corollary

3.11
, and
Lemma

5.16
justify this procedure. Write

\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}=\left[r_{1},\dots,r_{2n-2}\right],

and let
i_{j}
be the index of the skip corresponding to
r_{j}
. Consider
r_{\ell}=(\!(a,b)\!)
and
r_{\ell+1}=(\!(b,c)\!)
with
a<b<c
. Then we have the following cases (each one corresponds to a case in
Figure

6
):

(a)

If
r_{\ell}
and
r_{\ell+1}
are both the left ends of their pairs, then

•

i_{\ell+1}-i_{\ell}=c-b
if
(c\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)>(b\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)

•

i_{\ell+1}-i_{\ell}=c-b-1
if
(c\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)<(b\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)

(b)

If
r_{\ell}
is the right end of its pair and
r_{\ell+1}
is the left end of its pair, then

•

i_{\ell+1}-i_{\ell}=c-a
if
(c\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)>(a\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)

•

i_{\ell+1}-i_{\ell}=c-a-1
if
(c\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)<(a\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)

(c)

If
r_{\ell}
and
r_{\ell+1}
are both the right ends of their pairs, then

•

i_{\ell+1}-i_{\ell}=b-a
if
(b\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)>(a\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)

•

i_{\ell+1}-i_{\ell}=b-a-1
if
(b\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)<(a\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)

(d)

If
r_{\ell}
is the left end of its pair and
r_{\ell+1}
is the right end of its pair, then
c=a+n
and
i_{\ell+1}-i_{\ell}=n-1
.

The sequence of run-leaf labels and edges visited during the clockwise walk around
T
is:

\displaystyle\big[2,(\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{.8}{10}}2),9,(2\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{.8}{10}}),3,(\raisebox{0.15pt}{\scalebox{.8}{10}}5),2,(57),9,(75),3,(51),2,(13),9,(31),1,(14),9,

(reproduced below):

7.
Enumeration

In this section we prove Cayley’s formula for the number of vertex-labeled trees:

\left|\textsc{tree}_{n}\right|=n^{n-2}.

Our proof uses the bijection between
\textsc{tree}_{n}
and
\textsc{sub}_{n}
from
Theorem

6.1
, along with representation-theoretic techniques (previously obtained in a collaboration between the last author with P. Galashin and T. Lam) to compute the number of points in a particular braid variety
R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
over the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
q
elements. Using a trace formula due to Opdam and an identity due to Haglund, we obtain that

\left|R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|=(q-1)^{2n-2}[n]_{q}^{n-2},

where
[n]_{q}\coloneqq\frac{q^{n}-1}{q-1}
is the usual
q
-analogue.
Certain
distinguished subwords

\mathcal{D}_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}
index the Deodhar components of this braid variety
D_{\mathsf{u}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
:

R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\bigsqcup_{\mathsf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}}D_{\mathsf{u}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),

but the maximal distinguished subwords in
\textsc{sub}_{n}
are the only components that contribute to the sum when
q
is sent to
1
:

|\textsc{sub}_{n}|=\left((q-1)^{-2n+2}\left|R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|\right)\Bigg|_{q\to 1}=n^{n-2}.

7.1.
Braid varieties

The usual definition of braid varieties extends to the context of Kac-Moody groups (for simplicity, we give a specialization of the more general definition). For
\mathsf{w}=[s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m}]
a word in the simple reflections
S
of the Weyl group
W
, we denote this
braid variety
(over a finite field) by
R_{\mathsf{w}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. In slightly more detail, a split minimal Kac-Moody group
G
is associated to a symmetrizable generalized Cartan matrix; it is generated by a split torus
T
and root subgroups
U^{\pm}=\{U_{\pm\alpha_{i}}\}
. We have opposite Borel subgroups
B^{\pm}
generated by
T
and
U^{\pm}
, we have the flag variety
\mathcal{B}=G/B^{+}
with its decomposition into Schubert cells
\mathcal{B}_{w}=B^{+}\cdot{w}B^{+}/B^{+}
and opposite Schubert cells
\mathcal{B}^{w}=B^{-}\cdot{w}B^{+}/B^{+}
, and we can speak of the
relative position
of two flags
B_{1},B_{2}\in G/B^{+}
(written
B_{1}\xrightarrow{w}B_{2}
for
w\in W
). Then for
\mathsf{w}=[s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{m}]
with
w=s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{m}\in W
, we have

R_{\mathsf{w}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\left\{B^{+}=B_{0}\xrightarrow{s_{1}}B_{1}\xrightarrow{s_{2}}B_{2}\cdots\xrightarrow{s_{m}}B_{m}:B_{m}\in\mathcal{B}^{e}\right\}.

A
distinguished subword

\mathsf{u}
of
\mathsf{w}
is a subword for which a simple reflection must be used if it causes the current product to decrease in weak order—that is, if
u_{(j)}s_{j}<u_{(j)}
then
u_{j+1}=s_{j}
(see also
Corollary

5.15
). Write
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{w}}
for all distinguished subwords. By a natural extension of
[
Deo85
]
(see also
[
BH21
]
), the braid variety
R_{\mathsf{w}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
has a Deodhar decomposition into

R_{\mathsf{w}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\bigsqcup_{\mathsf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{w}}}D_{\mathsf{u}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),

where each
D_{\mathsf{u}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times})^{s(\mathsf{u})}\times\mathbb{F}_{q}^{t(\mathsf{u})}
, where
s(\mathsf{u})
is the number of skips of
\mathsf{u}
and
t(\mathsf{u})
is half the number of takes.

Let
B_{W}
be the
braid group
for
W
with generators
T_{i}
for each
s_{i}\in S
, and let
H_{W}=B_{W}/(T_{i}^{2}=(q-1)T_{i}+q)
be the
Hecke algebra
, with usual basis
\{T_{w}\}_{w\in W}
. Write
w=s_{1}\cdots s_{m}\in W
. By the same arguments as
[
KL79
, Lemmas A3 and A4]
and
[
GLTW22
, Corollary 5.3]
the number of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-points in the braid variety
R_{\mathsf{w}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is given by the trace

\left|R_{\mathsf{w}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|=q^{\ell(w)}\mathrm{tr}(T_{\mathsf{w}}^{-1}),

where for
X\in H_{W}
,
\mathrm{tr}(X)
returns the coefficient of the basis element
T_{e}
indexed by the identity.

7.2.
Opdam’s trace formula

We now specialize to
G
the affine Kac-Moody group of type
A_{n-1}
. Write
\Phi^{+}
for the positive roots of
\mathrm{GL}_{n}
,
Q=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}
for its
root lattice
,
Q^{+}\subset Q
for the positive span of the simple roots, and
\Lambda
for the weight lattice. Write
\widehat{S}_{n}
for the
extended affine symmetric group
, whose elements can be thought of as bijections
\widehat{w}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
such that
\widehat{w}(i+n)=\widehat{w}(i)+n
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{w}(i)=\binom{n+1}{2}\;\mathrm{mod}\;n
; it contains the elements of
\Lambda
as translations.

Given
\lambda\in Q^{+}
, we express
\lambda
in the basis of fundamental weights as
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}\lambda_{i}
and define
\lambda_{+}=\sum_{i:a_{i}>0}a_{i}\lambda_{i}
and
\lambda_{-}=-\sum_{i:a_{i}<0}a_{i}\lambda_{i}
.

Definition 7.1
.

A
Kostant partition

(a_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}
for
\lambda\in Q^{+}
is a sequence of nonnegative integers indexed by positive roots such that
\lambda=\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}a_{\alpha}\alpha
. We denote the set of all Kostant partitions for
\lambda
by
K(\lambda)
.

Opdam proved the following formula for the trace in the Hecke algebra
\widehat{H}_{n}
for the extended affine symmetric group.

Theorem 7.2

(
[
Opd03
, Cor. 1.18]
)
.

Let
[k]_{q}=\frac{(q-1)^{2}}{q}\frac{q^{k}-q^{-k}}{q-q^{-1}}
. For
\lambda=\lambda_{+}-\lambda_{-}\in Q^{+}
,

\mathrm{tr}(T_{{\lambda_{-}}}^{\phantom{-1}}T_{{\lambda_{+}}}^{-1})=q^{(\ell({\lambda_{-}})-\ell({\lambda_{+}}))/2}\sum_{(a_{\alpha})\in K(\lambda)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\\
a_{\alpha}>0\end{subarray}}[a_{\alpha}]_{q}.

7.3.
Haglund’s identity

In
[
Hag11
]
, Haglund proved a remarkable formula for the bigraded (in
x
- and
y
-degree) Hilbert series of the quotient ring of diagonal coinvariants. Haglund stated the formula in terms of
Tesler matrices
, which are a simple combinatorial rephrasing of Kostant partitions.

Theorem 7.3

(
[
Hag11
, Corollary 1]
)
.

Write
[k]_{q,t}=(q-1)(1-t)\frac{q^{k}-t^{k}}{q-t}
and
let
\lambda_{n}\coloneqq n\lambda_{n-1}\in Q^{+}_{n}
. Then

\mathrm{Hilb}(\mathrm{DH}_{n-1};q,t)=\left(\frac{1}{(q-1)(t-1)}\right)^{n-1}\sum_{(a_{\alpha})\in K(\lambda)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{n}\\
a_{\alpha}>0\end{subarray}}[a_{\alpha}]_{q,t}.

7.4.
Cyclic enumeration

Theorem 7.4

(P. Galashin, T. Lam, N. Williams)
.

\left|R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|=(q-1)^{2n-2}[n]_{q}^{n-2}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt|\textsc{sub}_{n}|=n^{n-2}.

Proof.

Since
[k]_{q}=[k]_{q,q^{-1}}
, we can use Opdam’s
Theorem

7.2
and specialize Haglund’s
Theorem

7.3
to conclude that

\left|R_{{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|=q^{\ell(\lambda_{n})}\mathrm{tr}(T_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}^{-1}})=(q-1)^{2n-2}[n]_{q}^{n-2}.

Since all maximal distinguished subwords have exactly
2n-2
skips and all other distinguished subwords have more than
2n-2
skips, we have

\displaystyle(q-1)^{2n-2}[n]_{q}^{n-2}=\left|R_{{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}}\left|D_{\mathsf{u}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|=\sum_{\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}}\left|D_{\mathsf{u}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|+\sum_{\mathsf{u}\not\in\textsc{sub}_{n}}\left|D_{\mathsf{u}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}}(q-1)^{2n-2}q^{(n-1)(n-2)/2}+\sum_{\mathsf{u}\not\in\textsc{sub}_{n}}(q-1)^{s(\mathsf{u})}q^{t(\mathsf{u})},

where
s(\mathsf{u})>2n-2
for all
\mathsf{u}\not\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
. Dividing by
(q-1)^{2n-2}
and letting
q\to 1
gives
|\textsc{sub}_{n}|=n^{n-2}
.
∎

Corollary 7.5

(Cayley’s formula)
.

|\textsc{tree}_{n}|=n^{n-2}.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Theorems

7.4
and
6.1
.
∎

8.
Future Work

8.1.
Distinguished subwords

It would be interesting to give a combinatorial interpretation for
all
distinguished subwords of
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
. For
n=2,3,4,5
, the number of such subwords is
1,4,45,1331
; this sequence does not appear in the Online Encyclopedia of Integer Sequences.

8.2.
Other weights

There should be a Fuss–Catalan extension
[
Wil23
]
, using the translation

\lambda_{m,n}=(m(n-1)+1)\lambda_{n-1}-(m-1)\lambda_{1}.

Maximal distinguished subwords will still be parameterized by trees, but the combinatorics of the run-leaves will be more complicated—the number of maximal subwords will be
(m(n-1)+1)^{n-2}
.

Much more generally
[
AGH+12
, Conjecture 7.1]
, there should be interesting combinatorics coming from the weight

\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}a_{i}\alpha_{i}\text{ with }a_{1}>a_{2}>\cdots>a_{n-1}\geq a_{n}=0.

In this case, the number of maximal distinguished subwords is
[
AGH+12
]

\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\big((i+1)a_{i}-ia_{i+1}\big).

8.3.
Relation to Galashin-Lam-Trinh-Williams

In this section we explore the possibility of a relationship between
R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and the rational noncrossing parking functions (and their braid varieties) of
[
GLTW22
, Section 8.5]
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
. We say that a skip in
\mathsf{u}
is a
negative
if the corresponding inversion
(\!(a,b)\!)
in
\mathsf{r}^{\mathsf{u}}
satisfies
a<b
and
1\leq(b\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)<(a\;\mathrm{mod}\;n)\leq n
. A skip is
positive
if it is not negative.

Example 8.2
.

The negative skips are colored purple in
Figures

1
and
5
, while the positive skips are left in white. Observe that there is exactly one negative skip in each column and each row except the last.

Proposition 8.3
.

Each
\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}
has exactly one negative skip in each column and each row except the last.

Proof.

It follows from
Corollary

3.11
and
Lemma

5.16
that the pair of negative and positive skips
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{1}
,
\mathsf{r}^{k}_{2}
appear in column
k
.
Suppose that
t_{1}=(\!(\bar{a}_{0},a_{1})\!)
, where
1\leq a_{1}<a_{0}\leq n
, is the inversion of the first negative skip in some row of our grid. The next skip has inversion
t_{2}=(\!(a_{1},a_{2})\!)
or
(\!(\bar{a}_{1},a_{2})\!)
, where
1\leq a_{2}\leq n
. If
t_{2}
is in the same row as
t_{1}
, then its column number must be greater than
a_{1}
. It follows that
a_{2}>a_{1}
, so
t_{2}=(\!(a_{1},a_{2})\!)
. If the next skip is again in the same row, then its column number must be greater than
a_{2}
, so its inversion is
t_{3}=(\!(a_{2},a_{3})\!)
, where
a_{2}<a_{3}\leq n
. Continuing in this way, we see that there cannot be another negative skip in this row.

It remains to show that the last row of the grid cannot contain a negative skip. The last skip cannot be negative because its inversion is
(\!(a,n)\!)
by
Corollary

3.11
. Moreover, every skip in the last row of the grid must be the second in its pair since the pairs occur in the same column. So if there is a negative skip with inversion
(\!(\bar{a}_{0},a_{1})\!)
in the last row, it must be in column
a_{0}
and the next skip must be in column
a_{1}
, which contradicts
a_{1}<a_{0}
. So there cannot be a negative skip in the last row.
∎

We will not recall the definitions of the rational noncrossing parking braid varieties
R_{\mathsf{c}^{n+1}}^{(w)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
here, instead referring the interested reader to
[
GLTW22
]
. We will simply describe how to use
Proposition

8.3
to conjecturally break our braid variety
R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
into pieces that should match the individual components of the noncrossing parking braid varieties (indexed by
w\in S_{n}
).

Remark 8.4
.

Minh-Tâm Trinh has constructed certain braid variety variants that bundle together the individual parking braid variety components by enriching the usual definition of braid variety by elements of the unipotent subgroup of
B^{+}
. There should be an isomorphism between
R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and this variant for the symmetric group
S_{n-1}
and the braid
\mathbf{c}^{n}
, where
\mathbf{c}
is the lift of any standard Coxeter element in
S_{n-1}
to its braid group.

For
w\in S_{n-1}
, write
\textsc{sub}_{n}(w)
for the set of all subwords
\mathsf{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}
with negative skips in the positions of the ones in the
(n-1)\times(n-1)
permutation matrix of
w
, and takes in the positions corresponding to the inversions of
w
(indices to the left of and above the ones). Write

R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}^{(w)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\bigsqcup_{\mathsf{u}\in\textsc{sub}_{n}(w)}D_{\mathsf{u}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}).

Example 8.5
.

If we fix
w=[1,3,2]\in S_{3}
,
\textsc{sub}_{4}(w)
consists of all distinguished subwords that must use skips in the purple boxes and takes in the green boxes of

\begin{array}[]{|C{2em}|C{2em}|C{2em}|}\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering\\
\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&&\\
\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

.
Then
\textsc{sub}_{4}(w)
contains three maximal distinguished subwords, and eight distinguished words in total:

\begin{array}[]{|C{2em}|C{2em}|C{2em}|}\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering\\
\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&&\\
\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering\end{array}\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{array}[]{|C{2em}|C{2em}|C{2em}|}\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering\\
\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&&\\
\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering\end{array}\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{array}[]{|C{2em}|C{2em}|C{2em}|}\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&&\\
\hline\cr$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&$\@add@centering&&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Conjecture 8.6
.

We have a disjoint decomposition

R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\bigsqcup_{w\in S_{n-1}}R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}^{(w)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}).

Moreover, for
w\in S_{n-1}
and
\mathsf{c}_{n-1}=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-2}]
, we have an isomorphism

R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}^{(w)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\simeq(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times})^{n-1}\times\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\ell(w)}\times R_{\mathsf{c}_{n-1}^{n}}^{(w)}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),

where
R_{\mathsf{c}_{n-1}^{n}}^{(w)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
are the noncrossing parking braid varieties of
[
GLTW22
]
.

Remark 8.7
.

When
w
is the identity of
S_{n-1}
, the subwords in
\textsc{sub}_{n}(e)
skip all instances of the affine reflection
s_{0}
in
\bm{\lambda}_{n}
and there are no required takes (since the identity has no inversions). Writing
\mathsf{c}_{n}=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}]
, we immediately have

R_{\bm{\lambda}_{n}}^{(e)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\simeq(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times})^{n-1}\times R_{\mathsf{c}_{n}^{n-1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),

where
R_{\mathsf{c}_{n}^{n-1}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is the Fuss-Dogolon braid variety for
S_{n}
, which can easily be shown to be isomorphic to the usual Catalan braid variety
R_{\mathsf{c}_{n-1}^{n}}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
in
S_{n-1}
.
